Season 3, Episode 20: The Express Service

Daniel watched the Noxian harbor shrink behind the Narrow Mercy, then looked at the replacement pump lashed to its deck.

“Three weeks,” he said. “We're giving him three weeks.”

Arthur finished tying his purse. “Giving who?”

“Heisenberg. We follow his repairmen all the way to Zaun, help deliver his shit, then rob him after he's had time to fix whatever's broken.”

Pell, the fitter beside the pump, raised a finger. “I don't know that it's still broken.”

“Exactly. Could be getting better right now. That's upsetting.”

The little simian took a drink from his frosted bottle. His black tail curled around the rail while he considered the coastline, pale eyes narrowed against the sun.

They'd boarded to follow a delivery into Heisenberg's business. That had been a useful prospect before Pell mentioned what the machinery was replacing. Now Daniel could see a quicker way to become somebody else's problem. I'll give the lad this: drink had never prevented him from recognizing an opportunity. Occasionally it encouraged him to recognize three where there was only a staircase, but today he had something.

Kaze turned from the water. “You want to go ahead.”

“I want my money before I grow a relationship with this fucking pump.”

“You paid for the voyage.”

“And I will carry that pain into every decision I make.”

Arthur approached Pell. “You been told where to report?”

“Rivermark receiving works. Lower freight road. Red water tower over the yard. They told us to ask for Dessa.”

“You met her?”

“No. Hiring office gave us the name.”

Chris spoke through the banjo Billy carried. Chris was inside Billy's peculiar interior home, watching the deck through the workshop's live feed; the enormous catfish man was the one physically standing beside Pell.

“If they've still got product coming out, there'll be something to take. If they haven't, we find out why.”

“You planning on fixing it?” Kaze asked.

“Depends what I get for fixing it.”

Daniel pointed at the banjo. “See? He's already applying for a job. We gotta leave.”

Captain Harl listened without interrupting his helmsman, then took a scrap of shipping paper into the shelter of the cabin wall. He drew the coast, marked the headlands he used, and added the approach to Piltover's sea gates.

“This is directions, not a survey. Follow the land. Don't cut across water because my pencil made it look narrow.”

Daniel accepted it. “You coming straight down?”

“With my cargo and my crew. At my speed.”

“Good. Keep doing that.”

Harl looked at him.

“You have my wholehearted approval,” Daniel added.

“Keep that too.”

Briar had been listening from beneath the sail's shadow. She lifted her wood-backed portrait out of the folded canvas protecting it.

“I'm coming.”

Arthur nodded. “Same arrangement.”

He would take sixty percent of the coming score; the others would divide the rest. Briar had agreed to participate. Traveling with the Sins hadn't made her a member, but it hadn't made her luggage either.

“And my twenty first,” Daniel said.

“I remember your twenty.”

“I like hearing it.”

Billy regarded the shore with growing satisfaction.

“Gentlemen, I believe this departure calls for a swimmer.”

“How good?” Harl asked.

Billy's smile spread.

“Captain, you have been very hospitable. I shall spare you the full answer.”

He took the banjo and the protected picture inside first. Then he offered passage to Arthur, Briar and Kaze. Each agreed before that vast mouth opened; each vanished through the actual entrance into the home sheltered within him.

Daniel stepped onto his cloud above the rail.

“Race you to that point.”

Billy followed his finger toward a distant shoulder of rock.

“You may begin whenever you feel sufficiently encouraged.”

Daniel was already gone.

Billy eased over the side, fell clear of the hull and entered the sea with a broad, heavy splash.

Below the surface, he drove his limbs through the water. His immense body met none of the drag that ought to have held it back. Each stroke added speed until the ship became a pale blur behind him. He angled toward the point, pushing against the water to turn, then drove hard in the opposite direction to shed his momentum before the rocks.

He surfaced beside a broad stone shelf.

Daniel arrived overhead a moment later, crouching on his cloud.

“I was checking the route.”

“An invaluable service.”

“Somebody has to be responsible.”

Inside, Arthur had barely finished looking around the living platform when Billy announced their arrival. Meatwad passed the camera to Charles Manson, then stood beside a chair pulled directly into the visitors' path. The little meat creature seemed delighted to have passengers.

“Welcome to Inside Job,” Meatwad said. “Was your trip comfortable?”

“Short,” Arthur said.

Charles followed him with the lens. “Would you say that speed changes a man's relationship with freedom?”

“I'd say you're standing in the door.”

Meatwad looked at Charles.

“That means move.”

Billy let the travelers out onto the rock. Briar received her portrait and checked its corners. Kaze stepped well clear of the water, studying the next rise of land. Arthur shaded his eyes as Daniel's cloud settled nearby.

The shared exterior changed. Billy withdrew into the persistent interior, and Chris stood outside in his place, wearing his headlamp and carrying the banjo's outward outlet. He had finally received the twelve silver Billy had been holding for him.

Chris looked over the level ground, took out a small red-and-white ball, and opened it toward the empty space.

A blue Subaru appeared on the rock, its gold wheels catching the afternoon light.

Arthur stopped walking.

“That yours?”

“Yep.”

“Been in that little thing?”

“Yep.”

Arthur looked at the ball, then walked around the car. He examined a tire, the dark glass and the space beneath the front bumper.

“Where's the part that pulls it?”

Chris opened the driver's door. “Engine does that. Burns fuel. Turns the wheels.”

Arthur leaned down to look at the pedals.

“And you tell it to stop with your feet.”

“One foot mostly.”

“Steer with that wheel?”

“You got it.”

Daniel came up behind Arthur. “Lot of horsepower in there.”

Arthur straightened slowly.

“You just said there weren't horses.”

Chris rubbed his jaw. “It's how they measure what the engine can do. No actual horses.”

Arthur looked toward the ball again.

“Son, you just took a wagon out of an egg. I wasn't ruling anything out.”

Daniel laughed, then caught Arthur's look.

“Fair.”

Chris showed Arthur the passenger door and the belt. Arthur pulled it once, testing the catch rather than pretending he understood it. Then he sat down, placed his hat in his lap and studied the windshield.

“Can I stop it from here?”

“You can ask real loud.”

“That ain't what I asked.”

Chris grinned. “No. You're trusting me.”

Arthur considered him, then shut the door.

The engine started with a deep, uneven growl that settled into a steady note. Chris drove along the broad track above the shelf, picking up speed gradually. Arthur's hand found the door as the car pressed him into the seat.

“Easy.”

Chris lifted his foot.

Arthur watched the bend approach. Chris braked, turned through it and brought them to a stop on a wider stretch of bare ground.

For a moment Arthur said nothing.

“No animal getting tired,” he said at last.

“Still gotta feed it.”

“But it won't bite me.”

“Wouldn't put your hand in the engine.”

Billy spoke through the banjo, which Chris had secured within hearing range. He was watching through the headlamp feed from inside.

“Quite a commendable little conveyance. Does it swim?”

“You just won swimming.”

“I was asking about versatility.”

Chris looked at Arthur. “You ready for the other part?”

“How much other part?”

The car's panels shifted. The familiar wheel and dashboard rearranged into flight controls as the blue body lengthened around them. Gold-edged wings unfolded from its sides, splitting into the recognizable cross of an X-wing.

Arthur stared out through the canopy.

“Chris.”

“Yeah?”

“Explain this before you do it.”

Chris did. Thrust, lift, controls, a place to sit and a place to land. He kept his hands still until Arthur had finished asking questions.

Then the engines rose, and the shore dropped away beneath them.

Arthur pressed his hat against his knee. His jaw tightened as the sea opened below the wing; after a while his hand loosened.

“Well,” he said softly. “Look at that.”

On the ground, Briar handed Kaze the wrapped portrait so he could secure it before lifting her.

“Don't crush it.”

“Wasn't planning to.”

“You weren't planning to crush me either. I'm saying both.”

He adjusted his hold beneath her knees and behind her back, keeping her restraints clear of his throat. She braced against him.

Kaze chose solid rock away from the others, bent his legs and launched.

The shelf burst beneath his feet. Stone fragments struck the water as he climbed in a long, violent arc over the coast. Briar's breath caught; her grip tightened. The ship was already far behind them.

Their descent carried them toward the top of a bare ridge. Kaze made a small adjustment in the air, landed with his legs absorbing the impact and carried the remaining momentum into several bounding steps. Rock cracked beneath him, but he kept Briar above the worst of the jolting.

She looked back at the sea.

“Again,” she said.

“You all right?”

“Yes. Go.”

Daniel's cloud passed above the ridge as Kaze gathered himself for the next leap.

They followed the coast through the remaining daylight: Chris holding an easy course and circling at agreed landmarks, Daniel ranging between him and the two figures crossing the high ground. They stopped together to compare Harl's directions with the land, eat and rest. Chris checked the vehicle's supplies before recalling it empty for the night.

After they ate, Daniel took his bottle to the edge of their shelter and sat with his feet over the rock. Kaze came out a little later and lowered himself beside him.

Far below, a ship's lantern moved slowly along the black water.

“Might be ours,” Daniel said.

“Miss your pump?”

“Don't start. We had something.”

Kaze picked a stone out of the sole of his black shoe. “You got an idea past arriving?”

“See what they have. See who wants to keep it. Try to leave richer.”

“That's Arthur's whole fucking religion.”

“Man's got results.”

Kaze tossed the stone over the edge. Daniel watched it fall until the darkness swallowed it.

“I don't want to spend three weeks waiting for somebody else's problem to finish,” Daniel said. “Then get there and find out we missed the good part.”

“Could be nothing waiting.”

“Then we go find something.”

Kaze looked at him, then out toward the lantern.

“Yeah. All right.”

Daniel took another drink. Neither spoke for a while. Behind them, Chris was explaining to Arthur that the guarded switch in the cockpit was not something to flip out of curiosity.

“I asked what it did,” Arthur said.

“Your finger was already on it.”

“Well, I was helping you identify the thing.”

Daniel smiled into the neck of his bottle.

At first light, Chris deployed the vehicle on clear ground again. Arthur climbed into the passenger place and secured his own belt this time. He watched the controls change as the car became the X-wing, keeping his hands well away from the guarded switch.

“You enjoyed that yesterday,” Chris said.

“Some of it.”

“Which part?”

Arthur glanced through the canopy at Daniel trying to fold Harl's paper against a gust of wind. The cloud beneath him had drifted several feet sideways while he wrestled with it.

“Being in here.”

That afternoon, Piltover's approach filled the horizon.

Above the industrial haze stood the prosperous city of merchants and inventors, its stone and metal catching the sunlight. Below it, Zaun's stacks breathed into the valleys between buildings. Lifts carried workers past pipes larger than some of the rooms they would sleep in. Somewhere down there, Heisenberg was turning sickness, appetite and paid muscle into a very successful business.

The Sins had been to Zaun before. They hadn't returned to admire the civic improvements.

Chris landed outside the busy approach roads. He and Arthur climbed out before he recalled the same vehicle. The flight had used fuel; folding it away hadn't put any back. Daniel joined them with Harl's creased directions, and Kaze set Briar down beside a low wall.

She took her portrait from him and flexed her fingers.

“Next time I want to see where we're landing.”

“You were looking backward.”

“There was a lot happening backward.”

Chris handed the outside to Billy. The portrait went back into protected storage with Briar's agreement, freeing her hands for the walk down the freight road.

Rivermark's red water tower rose over a brick yard between two taller factories. Its gate stood open. Wagons waited beside a loading platform, and men in stained work clothes carried sealed cases out of a warehouse.

Daniel stopped across the road.

“Let me ask.”

Arthur glanced at the bottle. “You gonna look for employment too?”

“I'm gonna look employed. Very different burden.”

Kaze looked through the open gate. “And when somebody asks you to fix something?”

“I'll complain about the last shift.”

Billy gave a thoughtful nod. “Convincing already.”

Daniel crossed alone, spear resting over one shoulder. A guard at the entrance watched his bare feet, his tail and finally his face.

“Deliveries round the side.”

“Dessa. Replacement pumps.”

The guard's expression changed.

“Where?”

“On a ship. I'm faster.”

The guard looked past him at the road, found no machinery and called into the yard.

A broad-shouldered woman came down from the loading platform, wiping grease off her fingers. Her hair was bound beneath a faded scarf. She looked tired enough to resent every syllable this conversation might require.

“I'm Dessa. Where's my crew?”

“Narrow Mercy. Four fitters. Pell's one.”

“And you?”

“Daniel.”

“Pell hired you?”

“No.”

She stopped wiping her hands.

Daniel took a drink.

“I came to talk before they get here.”

Dessa studied him, then the strangers across the road.

“About what?”

“Buying what's ready.”

“From Heisenberg?”

“Is there somebody cheaper in the building?”

She didn't smile. “Those six cases of Purple Pain are already sold.”

The cases on the loading platform bore the same stenciled mark. One of the six stood open for inspection, its rows of purple vials cushioned in packing material. Dessa closed it before Daniel could approach. This was Heisenberg's drug, the strength and euphoria he sold with a dependence that kept customers returning. Dessa was worrying about fulfilling an order; Daniel was looking at something another buyer might pay for.

“Then what isn't?”

“Nothing until the line's running right. If you want to place an order, bring money and an introduction.”

“How wrong is running wrong?”

“You buying product or applying to fix a pump?”

Daniel looked back toward Billy, who stood with the others across the road.

“Fuck. It's contagious.”

The guard put a hand on his shoulder.

Daniel looked down at it, then up at the man's face.

“I'm leaving.”

The man let go. Daniel crossed back to the others without hurrying.

“Six cases, already sold. Line's still giving them trouble. She's running the yard, and she knows I know about the ship.”

Arthur glanced at the gate. “You see payment?”

“No. She shut the case before I could ask for a free sample.”

“You want to take it now?” Kaze asked.

Daniel watched a wagon driver tighten a strap over the platform's nearest stack.

“Yes.”

“She'll know exactly who did it.”

“She'll know anyway when six cases climb into Billy.”

Kaze looked at Billy, then laughed.

“Fair enough.”

Arthur checked the clear line between the gate and the road. “I can cover the entrance. You keep them cases intact.”

Briar had been watching the guards.

“If somebody runs?”

Daniel looked along the warehouse wall. A narrow side door opened onto an alley leading away from the yard.

“We need the stuff, not everyone in it. Come round that side with Kaze. Don't let them take it out behind us.”

“I can do that,” she said.

Billy rolled his shoulders. “And I shall make room.”

Daniel passed his bottle into the curl of his tail, freeing both hands. He watched the guard's sword hand and the open space beyond the gate before stepping off the curb.

When they crossed the road together, Dessa was still on the platform. She saw Arthur's hand near his holster and immediately pushed the nearest worker away from the cases.

“Back inside,” she told him.

Then she shouted for the guards.

Daniel entered first. The man who had touched his shoulder drew a heavy short sword. Another guard emerged beside the warehouse with a pistol already rising.

Arthur drew on him from the gate.

“Set it down.”

The pistol guard stopped. His eyes moved to Billy, who had to duck beneath the gate's crossbeam to enter the yard.

The swordsman decided to finish the smaller problem in front of him.

He swung at Daniel's neck.

Daniel sagged beneath the blade as though his knees had betrayed him. His spear slipped through his fingers, butt striking the ground behind his heel. Then his foot closed around the shaft and brought it across the guard's shins.

The man's legs went out. Before his back reached the stones, Daniel caught the spear in one hand and drove its butt into his breastbone.

The impact slammed him flat. Something broke beneath his coat. He lay struggling for a breath that wouldn't come properly.

Daniel's hands and forearms were solid ice now, pale and ridged against the dark fur above his elbows. He stepped past the fallen man.

A third guard burst from the warehouse carrying a two-handed iron maul. Broad leather straps fixed a metal brace along his arms and shoulders. He lowered his head and charged straight down the platform steps.

Dessa shouted, “Not the stock!”

He was already swinging.

Cold mist spilled around Daniel. His outline stayed at the foot of the steps while his real body slipped to the man's right, concealed within that brief veil.

The maul struck the ice double through the middle.

It exploded into glittering chunks. The guard stumbled through them, discovered there was no body beneath his weapon and twisted toward the movement beside him.

Daniel was close enough to smell the oil in his brace.

The guard dragged the maul across, trying to catch him with the shaft. Daniel's pale eyes followed the man's shoulder turning before the weapon reached him. He met the shaft with his left forearm. The ice compressed at the instant of contact, turning the blow upward as he stepped inside its arc.

His right palm drove into the man's ribs.

The guard flew backward into the warehouse door. Wood burst around his shoulders. The maul struck the platform and bounced down the steps while he fell among the splinters, chest crushed beneath the brace that had done nothing to stop the force reaching him.

He didn't get up.

Daniel stood where he'd struck him, frost drifting from his fingers. No weaving now. He looked toward the man holding the pistol.

The pistol went on the ground.

Arthur nodded. “Appreciate it.”

Aye, sensible fellow. A man ought to be allowed one good decision even if it arrives very fucking late.

Dessa backed into the warehouse and pulled a red alarm handle beside the door. A mechanical bell began hammering above the yard.

Arthur kept the surrendered guard covered. “Daniel.”

“Heard it.”

Daniel climbed the steps. Through the broken doorway he saw workers moving toward the rear exit. Dessa remained beside the alarm, one hand on a metal cashbox.

“Don't,” she said.

He looked at the box.

“Company money?”

“Money I need.”

He took it from the table. She caught the handle at the same time.

Daniel could have pulled her across the room. Instead he held it still and waited until she looked at the frozen hand beside hers.

Her fingers opened.

“He pays people to come back for things,” she said.

“I know.”

“Then you're stupider than you look.”

Daniel tucked the box under his arm.

“There's a lot of competition for that opinion.”

Outside, Billy had taken the first sealed case in both hands. His mouth opened around it, and the wood disappeared into protected storage. He took the next from the stack. Inside, Chris made sure the incoming cases remained together and clear of the living platform.

The bell kept ringing.

At the side alley, Kaze and Briar watched workers spill from the rear door. One man carried a small crate in both arms.

Briar stepped into his path.

“Put that down.”

He froze, then lowered it carefully. The lid shifted, revealing a kettle, a cup and a folded coat.

Briar looked at the contents.

“Oh. Take it.”

He did, running so hard the cup rattled against the kettle.

Kaze leaned against the wall, listening to the alarm. “You're very good at this.”

“I checked.”

“You did.”

A younger worker slipped past the far end of the alley and sprinted toward the main road empty-handed. Kaze watched him go. Then Billy called that the last case was aboard, and Kaze turned back to Briar.

“Time.”

Daniel emerged with the cashbox. The first guard was still alive, curled around his broken chest. Dessa knelt beside him as they withdrew, pressing a folded coat under his head.

She watched Daniel until the gate hid him.

Six cases and a box of money. A dead guard, a badly hurt one, and a yard full of people who would remember them. Heisenberg had lost stock; somebody who had paid for it was about to discover they had nothing coming. That bell would finish ringing long before the trouble did.

They left the freight road by a narrow service cut and gathered beneath an abandoned loading ramp. Billy admitted the others once they asked, keeping the haul concealed and shortening the party visible outside. Daniel came in last with the box.

Charles raised the camera.

Daniel pointed at him. “Give me a minute.”

Charles lowered it.

At the workbench, Chris examined one opened case without removing its vials. Daniel set the cashbox beside him and worked its lock open with a brief touch of his magic. The lid lifted over stacked silver.

Arthur counted while Billy continued along the service road outside.

“Two hundred seventy.”

Daniel held out his hand.

Arthur gave him twenty first.

“There. Your passage.”

Daniel shut his eyes for a moment. “I'm healing.”

“You were healed yesterday.”

“Different wound.”

Arthur divided the remaining two hundred fifty according to their agreement: one hundred fifty for himself and twenty each for the other five. Billy's share stayed separate for an actual handoff. Daniel put his forty away with the seventy he'd brought from the ship.

Chris closed the inspection case.

“Six of these, and we ain't sold a drop.”

“Don't count it as money yet,” Arthur said.

“I didn't. I counted it as six of these.”

Briar accepted her coins and went to check her picture. Kaze sat on the edge of the platform, looking at Daniel with a crooked smile.

“You still want a sample?”

Daniel took his own bottle from his tail. “I'm a loyal customer.”

Meatwad waited until he had taken a drink, then pointed toward the interview chair.

“Can we ask now?”

Daniel glanced at the camera and nodded. Charles handed it to Meatwad so he could sit beside him.

“What would you say brought about today's success?” Charles asked.

Daniel rested his bottle on his knee.

“Fear of steady employment.”

Chris laughed from the bench.

Charles leaned closer. “Would you describe yourself as a businessman?”

“Armed robber.”

Charles looked at the camera. “We can use the first answer.”

“You said the first answer,” Meatwad told him.

“He understood what I meant.”

Daniel leaned forward until he filled most of the picture.

“Armed. Robber.”

Chris leaned on the workbench, laughing again.

Charles reached toward the camera. “We ought to stop.”

Meatwad backed away, keeping Daniel in the frame.

“Why? He's finally explaining what y'all do.”

<!-- END LOGICAL OWNER: state/evidence/S3_Reset_Episode_20_20261018.txt -->
